These ownership arguments establish native component reconstruction, while complete table assembly, cross-catalog logical closure and fresh Engine activation remain separate obligations.

Named vector and evaluated-value collections transfer only future membership construction to the ordinary native mutation allowance at completion. Their original immutable name/source nodes and payload reservations remain owned by each surviving root. Thus input exhaustion or cancellation after completion cannot disable later registration, replacement or removal, while existing captured membership continues to select the same original child. The transfer changes neither an identity nor a membership edge and allocates no replacement population.

Native source membership constructs successors directly from immutable Core snapshots. For insertion or removal, Core clones only the root handle, reserves the selected path under the successor construction allowance, and shares unchanged original entries and subtrees. It never restores that mixed-ownership root into a mutable owner that requires one original allowance. The original mutable-restoration check is unchanged. This gives an induction over membership edits: each published generation has exactly its requested identity set, its old nodes retain old leases, its new nodes retain new leases, and failure publishes no generation.

## Destination-owned catalog mutation

SQL owns native declarations and authority; Execution owns comments, secondary-index metadata and paired foreign catalog publication. Each owner decodes its native generations. Let C be the ordered mapping from original keys to native definitions, including every declaration, bound plan, literal payload, identity and authority tuple. A native generation additionally owns immutable nodes, the reservations for their actual allocations and the allowance M selected for future construction. An image decoder admits its original nodes and payloads under input allowance I; `read_image_with_mutation_budget` supplies a distinct destination allowance M. The existing `read_image` is the special case M = I. These interfaces do not activate an Engine or certify cross-catalog closure.

The transition retains each decoded root and its cached footprint and replaces only the future-construction handle. Its projection onto C is therefore the identity: all lookups, iteration, images, existing query evaluation and authority observations are unchanged. The owning native mutation and comparison algorithms remain the same. Consequently transfer commutes with every read-only observation of C and introduces no new algebraic operator, rewrite or numeric approximation. Admission success for a later mutation is determined by M, as explicitly selected by the destination owner.

For a completed mutation, the existing producer first admits its selected path and changed definition under M, then publishes the successor. Unchanged nodes and definitions still own their I reservations. A captured predecessor shares its original entries and retains its separate capture admission; replacement or source retirement cannot substitute successor data. On failed admission, the existing publication boundary leaves the predecessor untouched and retires every candidate allocation. Induction over successive edits establishes exact native catalog behavior and lease lifetime for every retained generation, including when I is exhausted or its input cancellation token is cancelled. That token belongs only to decoding and is not installed in the runtime owner.

Trigger and rule input transfers each fully decoded declaration root to M before attaching it to its still-private relation root. Input construction of that outer root remains under I until complete EOF, then its future allowance also becomes M. Thus nested edits and relation publication select the same destination allowance without visiting, rebuilding or reevaluating unrelated declarations. A decode failure retires the entire private candidate. Routine and sequence wrappers apply the same transition to their native definition root; database authority retains its original admitted tuple and funds only changed successors through M. The transition allocates no additional allowance object and preserves exact existing input-allocation accounting.

Foreign definitions and authority are decoded together under I before either root is returned. Transfer selects M for both roots without changing their matching key sets. The existing paired publication operation constructs both successors before replacing the live catalog, so rejection at either allocation boundary leaves both original roots observable. Comments and secondary-index rows retain their original encoded payloads and object addresses by the same map argument; destination edits do not rerun catalog lookup, SQL binding or application callbacks.

## Admitted native source registration

Let a source be an original native value V, its capture domain R, immutable source identity i and birth b, and the registered allocation H containing that identity and the value lock. The value owns its variable payload independently. Ordinary registration and `register_with_control` use the same owning transition; the latter additionally admits the fixed registered allocation under input allowance I before moving V or publishing i. Repeating an existing registration in R preserves its original allocation and identity; a different domain is rejected. No source is rebound to a new identity or repaired by a caller.

The transition obtains the same recursive publication admission, reserves H, allocates i and observes input cancellation before taking V from its unregistered owner. Every fallible operation precedes that move. A quota, identity-allocation or cancellation error therefore leaves V and the unregistered state unchanged; a consuming constructor instead retires V with its existing owners. Publication moves V without copying or executing it. Domain, birth, selected-cut eligibility, first-read preservation, write admission and final retirement use the existing capture operations. Projecting away the admission carrier yields exactly the previous registration transition, so all stored-value observations and compositions of capture/read/write/retire operations are preserved.

An admitted H and its reservation are one shared owning allocation. A key promoted to a live reader retains that same allocation, including when the public source owner retires before the read completes. Destruction drops V before returning the reservation. Weak membership independently admits the fixed retained allocation through the existing key contract; it never retains future mutable payloads. A frozen result keeps the selected cut's separate reservation. These ownership paths cover every strong reader and retained weak membership without attaching a header token to an unrelated catalog field or Engine lifetime.

The construction cancellation signal is not stored in H. Completed native reads and mutations consequently retain their existing semantics after I is exhausted or cancelled; only new allocation admission changes. The destination mutation boundary still admits and publishes its own successors. Named vector and evaluated-index image input use this registration transition before publishing each original name and native source in the writable collection. The extension adds no query operator, optimizer rewrite or numeric approximation.

## Shared catalog generation ownership

Let V be a native catalog value with its existing ownership and Clone operation. A retained generation is N(A), an ordinary shared allocation of V, or I(A, H, k), the same native value with an admitted shared-header reservation H and independent identity k. Projection of either carrier is V. Payload reservations belong to V's native allocation owners before sharing; the carrier never accepts a separate payload reservation whose lifetime would depend on an arbitrary Clone implementation. SQL definitions, Execution registries and Engine adapters apply their existing operations to the projected value. No decoder, query operator, numeric approximation or catalog dependency rule is introduced here.

Cloning a retained generation shares A, H and k without cloning V. For N, mutation is exactly the previous Arc copy-on-write operation. For I, mutation first clones V into an ordinary successor allocation and then replaces that writer's carrier. Every surviving snapshot retains the original header. Payload lifetime follows V's existing native ownership: a shared admitted child retains its reservation through its own owner, and an independently copied child belongs to the successor. If cloning unwinds, replacement has not occurred and the original generation remains installed. Each successful mutation therefore has the same projected value transition as the existing native operation. Reads, snapshot clones, mutations and undo compose with the same observations; undo restores the carrier selected at the actual transaction boundary, including its identity and reservations.

Construction reserves the shared header before allocating it and moves the native value into that header. Quota rejection drops the consumed private value through its existing owners without publishing a generation. Field destruction releases the native value before returning the header reservation. Every strong snapshot retains H until the last reader of A retires. Construction does not retain a cancellation signal; subsequent writes use their existing operation boundary and native mutation allowance. This argument composes with SQL definition, table metadata, resource and destination-mutation ownership because the sharing interface neither separates a payload from its reservation nor substitutes an unrelated lifetime token.

A non-owning cache key observes allocation identity, not value equality. Native generations preserve the existing weak Arc identity. Admitted headers use a separate zero-sized shared identity whose weak allocation remains unique while the key exists; the key cannot read the value or retain its header or native payloads. Reference-count bookkeeping remains outside Core's existing payload accounting boundary. Shared generations have the same key; a detached successor has another key; a later allocation cannot reuse the retained key's identity. Routine-description invalidation thus preserves the previous aliasing test without holding a retired catalog alive. Strong definition caches retain the actual generation normally.

CatalogCell, table definition/metadata adapters, durable snapshots, portal snapshots and transaction undo retain this common carrier. SQL's named-definition view borrows the same native definitions, and Execution's catalog merges and derivation caches use the same value and allocation-identity operations. First-read capture still delegates to the existing source registry and produces a separately admitted frozen value. These adapters preserve declaration identities, lookup results, the generations selected for rollback, capture cuts and all defined compositions. They do not establish complete database closure or fresh Engine activation.

The type-erased index snapshot coerces the original shared allocation to the existing registry observation interface. Delegated cardinality and ordered seeks borrow the same V; no wrapper or row copy is allocated. Erased strong owners preserve H and V's native ownership through cloning and retirement by the same reference-count argument. H cannot retire merely because the concrete registry type is hidden.

Equality is exposed for Eq payloads and first compares the original shared allocation. A shared generation requires constant work and is equal by the payload's reflexivity law; distinct generations delegate to existing value equality. This preserves schema-dependency observations and the previous Arc fast path without assuming reflexivity for arbitrary PartialEq payloads.

## Writable table data assembly

The native owning interfaces are implemented in the current candidate. Engine assembly described in this section is preserved at source [`4a2ed04aba243d3443382e07baea787b480d9275`](https://github.com/cognica-io/uqa-engine/tree/4a2ed04aba243d3443382e07baea787b480d9275/crates/uqa-engine/src/backup/memory/restore) for the subsequent production restore unit under #1322; it is outside the current candidate and does not establish public restoration.

Analyzer input preserves the pair of historical compiled revisions for each field, independently of the current revision registered under either name. Its key, optional revision names and compiled owners retain their original decoding allocations. Copying an immutable field catalog shares those owners; renaming or replacing a binding uses the ordinary mutation operation. A restored lazy default shares its unresolved configuration owner, and construction performs no resource resolution. Therefore every explicit field lookup selects the same historical revision as the image, while an unbound field retains the existing first-use resolution behavior. Cancelling input after construction cannot change either selection, because the runtime resolver retains no input cancellation signal.

Let a table's evaluated data be the product $T=(D,I,V,K)$, where $D$ maps document identities to complete stored tuples, $I$ is the evaluated text corpus, $V$ maps field names to native vector owners, and $K$ maps named index identities to evaluated value caches with their historical comparison policy. The surrounding image reader establishes ordered component membership and complete metadata; the enclosing database restoration must separately establish definition, analyzer-resource and cross-catalog closure. The assembly operation is defined only on complete component images admitted by their existing native decoders. It neither interprets SQL nor changes any operator's carrier, ranking, numeric approximation or NULL semantics.

For each component, the existing image inverse and writable-owner arguments establish $decode(encode(x)) \equiv x$ for every defined native observation, including tuple metadata, text occurrences and normalization lengths, vector coordinate bits and physical topology, and evaluated values and policy. Assembly routes document and text units to their original addressed identities, vector units to their exact field names, and value units to the selected exact index name. Named value metadata must precede its values, each value must match that selected name, and completion requires the metadata's declared count. The enclosing reader supplies global address ordering; native builders reject duplicate registrations and row identities. Therefore the assembled product has exactly the original component-to-identity mapping. A projection to any component commutes with assembly and decoding. Any defined composition of these projections with existing relational, text, vector or value-index operations consequently has the same observations by substitution of component equality; this does not claim validity for a database whose catalog closure has not been established.

Completion returns the ordinary writable native owners. An application mutation is therefore the same owning operation applied to the same component state, with the same affected component identities. Induction on a finite sequence of successful native operations preserves the correspondence after each mutation. Existing snapshot and rollback operations retain or restore their original component roots, so the same induction applies to histories containing those operations. No rebuild, analyzer invocation, type rebinding or replacement of an approximate index with a different algorithm occurs during assembly. Subsequent ordinary mutations may perform their established work, including normal vector maintenance; their existing semantic and approximation contracts are unchanged.

Each input step consumes the previous unpublished candidate. Native values retain their decoding reservations, registration names and nodes receive admission before publication, and each completed child keeps the reservation attached to its actual native allocation. A decoding error, mismatched index identity, missing declared value, cancellation or unwind drops the consumed candidate and all preceding components before any table is published. A successful result retains the same payload owners while the input controller itself retires. Runtime operations use their existing cancellation and mutation boundaries, so cancelling a completed input cannot cancel a later application operation. These ownership transitions preserve both observation correspondence and failure atomicity without requiring a caller to repair the candidate.

Engine table input additionally requires the original metadata sequence and equal object/storage identities in the address and definition. It installs the native column, authority, constraint, hierarchy, analyzer, statistics and maintenance generations without executing their declarations. A complete table enters an unpublished registry whose ordered lookup map uses the same `RelationIdentity` comparison as ordinary lookup. Its exact owned nodes and original key buffers receive admission before allocation or transfer; immutable membership entries retain their own copied keys and source identities. Projection to the key-to-table function is unchanged, so lookup, sorted traversal, rename, insertion and removal have their previous meaning. Runtime copy-on-write successors own newly copied nodes and keys, while surviving original generations keep their original leases.

Capture publication retains disjoint source groups and weak child update addresses. Switching its future mutation allowance changes neither the groups nor their identities, and requires no population traversal. Each later replacement starts from the same retained member and source roots under the new allowance, publishes both roots together, and preserves the existing overlap and retirement checks. By induction over child replacements, removals and table publication, selected cuts remain exactly those of ordinary publication. Transaction undo restores the original immutable membership root without retaining an extra live table map. Failure while constructing an unpublished registry retires the whole candidate, including its source membership, names and previously reconstructed tables. This table-level correspondence does not establish cross-catalog dependency closure or authorize database activation.

## Ordered native database assembly

The native owning interfaces are implemented in the current candidate. Engine assembly described in this section is preserved at source [`4a2ed04aba243d3443382e07baea787b480d9275`](https://github.com/cognica-io/uqa-engine/tree/4a2ed04aba243d3443382e07baea787b480d9275/crates/uqa-engine/src/backup/memory/restore) for the subsequent production restore unit under #1322; it is outside the current candidate and does not establish public restoration.

Let a complete structural memory image be the ordered product $I=(H,C_1,\ldots,C_{24},F,R,T_1,\ldots,T_n)$, where $H$ contains the source and absolute catalog-counter position, $C_i$ is its named native catalog slot, $F$ is the ordered field-binding map, $R$ maps resource-kind/name pairs to native values, and each $T_i$ is the complete addressed table product defined above. The shared repository reader establishes framing, exact ordered inventory, component digests, required catalog/table prefixes and one physical generation per relation. Each component decoder establishes its own native validity. These premises do not establish cross-catalog dependency closure: no result of this assembler is an activation certificate, and no public restore capability follows from structural completion alone.

Define assembly $A$ by routing each image address to that component's owning inverse and installing its result at the same native identity. Catalog slots are total only after slots 1 through 24 have arrived. Sequence metadata at slot 19 is an unpublished continuation; slot 20 supplies authority, and completion requires equal cardinality and matching relation/persistence for every record. Thus neither half alone denotes a sequence catalog. Field bindings preserve their independently recorded historical index/search revisions. Graphs, models, paths and named analyzers are selected by the exact kind/name pair. A table enters native membership only after its definition sequence and evaluated data are complete. For a structurally valid image, induction on its ordered components shows that every completed prefix has exactly the native values of that prefix, no duplicate public identities, and at most the next unfinished table or sequence continuation. At EOF, each component projection of $A(I)$ therefore equals its native decoding.

Every component codec's preceding inverse argument supplies $decode(encode(x)) \equiv x$ for its defined observations. Substituting those equalities into the product construction proves componentwise preservation of catalogs, authority tuples, evaluated graph/path state, model weights, historical analyzers and complete table data. For a database whose separate dependency validation succeeds, an existing operation receives the same relevant projections, so its defined observation is preserved. This implication explicitly assumes complete dependency closure; it does not infer closure from equal component counts or valid byte framing. Assembly invokes no stored SQL, trigger, model training, text analysis or vector rebuilding. Model numeric representations and graph/vector approximation contracts remain those of their original owning codecs, with no new numeric approximation or optimizer rewrite.

The repository codec bridge temporarily owns both the current address and payload reader. It restores them only after the codec succeeds and reaches verified EOF. A stored repository read failure takes precedence even if a codec ignores its I/O error. Failure or unwinding drops the payload owner. The Engine input transition takes its entire unpublished candidate and reader before work, restoring them only after success; errors, denied allocation and cancellation therefore retire the previous native prefix and repository lease together. Successful structural completion returns only the private state product. The step is bounded for repository verification but consumes a complete native codec unit; a bounded public job must supply its additional scheduling contract separately.

Native model adoption stores the original admitted model inside a shared immutable handle, so every alias either retains that same payload reservation or makes an ordinary independent deep copy. Original names remain paired with their immutable map entries. Graph publication changes roots in place while retaining the reservation for its existing header allocation; separately cloned headers use their ordinary native ownership. Nested graph membership, adjacency and label maps select the current writer's allowance when constructing a successor, while unchanged immutable roots keep their original leases. These transitions change allocation ownership for future work without changing any resource value or identity. Consequently the componentwise correspondence is preserved through ordinary replacement, rollback, capture and parent retirement.

For a graph transaction, let $G$ denote the selected native generation, $A$ its enclosing handle allocation and $H$ the reservation of $A$. The transaction retains $G$ as an immutable rollback checkpoint and temporarily owns $H$ outside the callback-mutable value. Every callback replacement therefore leaves $H$ live while $A$ is live, including nested transactions and a change between memory and persistent handles. On success the final value receives $H$; on an error or unwind the original handle and $G$ are restored before $H$ returns to that allocation. Persistent operations complete or roll back their existing storage checkpoint before that state transition. Cloning a value shares its native payload roots but does not duplicate the reservation for a different enclosing allocation. Hence each transition retains exactly the original header reservation, never requires a second claim from the exhausted input allowance, and preserves the ordinary commit/rollback projection to graph observations. The outermost boundary owns $H$ throughout nested operations, so induction on nesting proves the same property for any finite transaction history. Final destruction releases $H$ after the retained value, while separately retained immutable generations keep their own payload owners.


For a resident or spilled HNSW map, write the state as $(K,V,P,B)$: ordered keys, native values, optional immutable physical pages and future construction allowance. The transfer replaces only $B$; $K$, $V$, $P$ and every existing allocation lease are unchanged. Resident successors reserve new paths under the destination child allowance; spilled successors append through the existing map operation using the destination workspace. Fixed page caches keep their existing reservation. Unused conversion workspace from the input writer is discarded, preventing later growth against that retired allowance. DiskANN applies the same transformation to canonical/change roots and retains its existing physical generation, while completed query/origin controls use the independent destination lifetime. Existing mutation and query algorithms are unchanged, so induction over a finite successful mutation history preserves the same native observations, including retained snapshots. Input exhaustion or cancellation after successful reconstruction cannot constrain later work through a retained construction control; ordinary destination limits and failures still apply.

The captured catalog counter is an absolute position, including uninitialized state, wrap history and exhaustion. Fresh concurrency construction installs that counter while initializing new session, transaction and lock state. For every initialized position $p$, the next ordinary OID operation uses the same successor and wrap function as the captured counter; an uninitialized counter retains its existing first-allocation rule, and an exhausted counter still fails. No live counter or transaction owner is reset. This preserves future catalog identity allocation while separating the restored database's transaction history from its source.

## Native authority dependency validation

Authority validation is a read-only predicate over the already decoded catalog product. Let $U$ be the live role map, keyed by public OID with an immutable incarnation in each value. The SQL-owned authority index admits its ordered nodes before construction, rejects duplicate OIDs and incarnations, and preserves the reserved bootstrap identity. A live endpoint $(o,i)$ resolves exactly when $U[o].incarnation=i$. Names are not lookup keys at this boundary, so renaming preserves an endpoint while dropping and recreating a role with the same name or OID cannot acquire its old ownership or privileges. Engine constructs an unpublished runtime through its ordinary constructor, supplies an immutable admitted catalog view to Execution, and returns that private runtime only after the authority predicate succeeds. Other dependency predicates and public activation remain separate obligations.

For each privilege bit $p$, let $G_p$ contain an edge from grantor $a$ to grantee $b$ precisely when an ACL entry grants $b$ the grant option for $p$. The owner initially has every grant option. Define $R_p$ as the least set containing the owner and closed under $G_p$. A grant of privilege $p$ is supported exactly when its grantor belongs to $R_p$. The implementation propagates newly reached privilege bits through admitted adjacency lists; initially only the owner is reached, and each propagation follows an existing edge carrying the same bit, proving soundness by induction. Conversely, induction on the length of an owner-rooted path shows every reachable endpoint is eventually queued and reached. Termination follows because a role's finite bitset only grows. Thus the computed sets equal $R_p$, including graphs with multiple paths or cycles, and disconnected grant cycles cannot create authority. Every retained bit causes at most one further propagation from an endpoint, bounding edge visits by the fixed privilege width; ordered lookup adds logarithmic map work rather than repeated scans of the complete ACL population.

Each ACL independently rejects duplicate grant paths, empty privileges, grant options without their privilege and PUBLIC grant options. Every owner, grantor and non-PUBLIC grantee must resolve in $U$. Relation ACLs supply additional grant-option edges when checking one column ACL, matching the existing rule that a table-level grant option can authorize a column grant. Duplicate-path scope remains the individual ACL, and relation-only privileges cannot appear in a column ACL. Column ACL entries and their retained tuple revisions must name a public attribute of the accompanying immutable relation descriptor. System relations use their existing inline bootstrap ACLs and controlled column-name producer. Object, schema, sequence and database authority use the same graph predicate with their original independent privilege bits, so one privilege never supplies a different privilege's grant option. The graph is validation workspace only: absent and empty ACLs, original entry order and all stored tuple revisions remain unchanged.

Role membership has a different retained-reference contract from object ACLs. A deleted target or member incarnation may remain in a stored membership and must not be rebound through a reused name or OID; the membership grantor must still resolve in $U$. Native validation preserves those target/member identities, enforces the existing incarnation-to-OID consistency and bootstrap rules, and validates membership OID and key uniqueness. It forms a directed edge from member to role for every retained membership, including separate grantors, and applies topological removal with admitted indegrees and adjacency. Acyclic graphs eventually remove every vertex by induction on their topological order. If vertices remain, every remaining vertex has an incoming retained edge, so following predecessors in the finite remainder reaches a cycle. Therefore rejection is exactly the cyclic-graph case after endpoint validation, with no deletion, rewriting or accidental inheritance by replacement roles.

Execution applies these SQL-owned predicates to database and schema authority, ordinary and foreign table columns, views, sequences, user types, routines, built-in routine overrides, system relations and foreign definition owners from one immutable snapshot. It neither parses nor evaluates stored SQL, invokes a host callback, nor repairs a missing role. A successful predicate does not change any input component, so the preceding native-image correspondence remains an identity on all admitted observations and compositions. Validation failure releases the graph workspace without changing the supplied catalog. The Engine restore owner must retire a rejected candidate together with its decoded allocations before activation. Every graph construction and traversal checks its original cancellation and admits allocation before publication, so quota denial and cancellation cannot leave an activated or partially validated database. This establishes authority closure only; full type/relation/routine/resource closure and the bounded public restore lifecycle are still required before exposing the Engine.

## Native stored-plan relation validation

Let a stored plan be a finite native query or supported bound SQL command tree, and let $V$ be the multiset of CTE names visible at one lexical boundary. Define its concrete relation references structurally: a table source contributes its original name and inheritance flag unless the name is unqualified and belongs to $V$; mutation targets and operator-join inputs always contribute their original relations. Query roots, set operands, scalar-query arenas, source subqueries and command query inputs contribute the union of their children's references. Values and retained evaluated rows contribute none. This predicate does not inspect scalar function bindings, sequence-valued expressions, constraints or external resources; those have separate closure requirements.

For a nonrecursive WITH list, the first body sees the inherited $V$, and each subsequent body additionally sees all preceding names; the enclosing query or command sees the entire list. A recursive list makes all its names visible in each body and the enclosing query. Nested scopes increment name multiplicities and remove exactly their own increments on exit, so shadowing never removes an outer declaration. SQL's borrowed iterative traversal implements these transitions with an admitted task stack and name-count map. Induction over the scheduled tasks proves that a body enters with precisely this visibility set, every relational child is visited once through its owning edge, and exit restores the caller's set. Qualified names, mutation targets and operator operands never pass through the CTE exclusion. The visitor therefore reports exactly the structurally defined concrete references, including their existing inheritance selection, without cloning or changing a plan.

Execution decodes each reported bound name into its original schema and local identifier without applying a search path or temporary alias. Its existing immutable relation namespace and graph-label owner must contain that identity. The check rejects a missing relation or noncanonical unqualified name. Stored relation references retain their existing name identity contract; no additional object incarnation is inferred. Bound SQL routine bodies use the same traversal, while deferred source bodies remain uncompiled. Unsupported stored command forms reject rather than silently skipping potential dependencies.

Success is the identity on the supplied plans, catalog declarations and native values. It consequently preserves the preceding image correspondence and every established relational, ranked, graph and transaction observation for states satisfying this predicate and the other activation preconditions. No query, trigger, callback or type input is executed. Failure releases scratch without changing the supplied catalog; the Engine restore owner must withhold and retire a candidate that fails the predicate. Original and invoking cancellation are checked during traversal; task, name and decoded-identity allocations use the supplied allowance. Payload size does not determine traversal scratch, and the visitor introduces no recursive Rust call stack. This component establishes stored-plan relation existence only; complete logical closure, persistent-provider integration and bounded public restoration remain required before activation.

## Native declared type reference validation

Let a selected catalog's concrete type address space be a partial map $T$ from `pg_type` OIDs to a kind and its original physical metadata. SQL constructs $T$ from the five native type registries and the row/array identities supplied by ordinary, foreign and view relation owners. Base and generated-array OIDs occupy the same address space; collisions across registries or with fixed builtin, pseudo-type and system-domain identities reject construction. A claim is installed only in private, admitted workspace, and failed construction drops the entire index. Lookup uses kind and OID, never a current display name or search path. Therefore a missing type, a different kind at the same OID, or an array address used as a scalar identity cannot satisfy a stored reference. Native reference formats whose identity is only an OID retain that existing identity contract; this validator does not invent an additional incarnation field.

For a concrete type occurrence, array wrappers recurse into their original element, enum references require the original array OID, composite references require both array and describing-relation OIDs, and range/multirange references require their original array OID and endpoint alignment. A domain occurrence must match its declaration's base-type OID, modifier, collation selection and explicit or legacy-derived array OID, then validates its nested base occurrence. Fixed system domains use the same native declaration producer as ordinary catalog typing. These checks neither replace nor reconstruct the occurrence, so a successful result is the identity on every existing type, modifier, declared value layout and display name.

Live concrete membership adds a directed edge from a domain, range, composite or relation row type to each user type directly contained in its live member declarations. Array wrappers preserve the element dependency; a range and its multirange share their endpoint dependency. Repeated fields retain independent edges, and dropped attributes are excluded because their historical descriptors no longer define live tuple members. Every edge endpoint is validated against $T$ first. Topological removal with admitted indegrees and adjacency removes all vertices exactly when the finite graph is acyclic: an acyclic nonempty remainder has a zero-indegree vertex, while a nonempty remainder with no such vertex contains a predecessor cycle. Thus direct or indirect recursive concrete layouts are rejected without expanding recursive descriptors. Each edge is inserted and removed once; ordered map access bounds the graph work by $O((|T|+|E|)\log(1+|T|))$ and admitted workspace by $O(|T|+|E|)$, in addition to visiting the supplied concrete type syntax.

Execution supplies live ordinary/foreign columns, standalone composite members, domain bases, range endpoints, materialized-view column types and stored routine declaration/default type identities from the same immutable snapshot. Original decoded values, historical dropped-field descriptors, deferred source bodies and ordinary declaration validation are untouched. SQL owns the identity and membership predicates. Successful validation preserves the preceding native-image correspondence, while failure releases validation workspace without changing the supplied declarations. The Engine restore owner must keep the candidate private until all required predicates succeed and retire its original reservations on rejection. Cancellation and allocation denial are checked during construction and traversal. This establishes the supplied declared-type reference predicate, not complete stored-expression, relation, routine, constraint or external-resource closure; those predicates and bounded public restoration remain separate activation requirements.

## Native inheritance reference validation

Let $R$ be the immutable map from canonical ordinary-table identities to their decoded hierarchy and persistence, and let $E$ contain one directed edge from each table to each declared parent in its original order. SQL builds an admitted index of borrowed definitions before visiting any edge. Every parent name must already be schema-qualified and resolve exactly in $R$; no search path or name rebinding is available. Repeated parents, repeated relation identities, absent parents and invalid explicit parent sequence membership reject the candidate. Empty legacy sequence arrays keep the existing declaration-order interpretation, while explicit positive unique sequence numbers retain their exact values and may have gaps.

A partition edge requires one parent whose original declaration is partitioned and whose strategy matches the child's stored bound form; HASH does not admit DEFAULT. Ordinary inheritance cannot use a partition or partitioned relation as its parent. Both paths reuse SQL's existing parent/child persistence predicate. These checks inspect borrowed metadata without evaluating partition keys or bounds, rewriting columns or repairing missing parents. They establish parent identity and edge-kind consistency; stored-expression typing, bound-value validity and sibling disjointness remain separate predicates.

The indexed graph has one admitted adjacency entry per declared edge and one incoming count per table. Removing a zero-incoming vertex decrements exactly the counts of the parents reached by its edges. By induction, each remaining count equals the number of incoming edges whose originating vertices have not yet been removed. A finite acyclic nonempty remainder has a zero-incoming vertex, whereas a nonempty remainder with none contains a predecessor cycle. Therefore all vertices are removed exactly when the supplied inheritance graph is acyclic. Traversal is iterative even for deep ancestry; each edge is inserted and removed once, with ordered identity lookup giving $O((|R|+|E|)\log(1+|R|))$ work in addition to name decoding and $O(|R|+|E|)$ admitted workspace.

Execution supplies the complete ordinary-table registry from one snapshot, and the private Engine assembler invokes this predicate before returning its candidate. Success changes neither an original declaration nor any input owner, so previously established image correspondences and existing inheritance/partition observations are preserved. Cancellation, missing membership and denied allocation release the entire private validation workspace; the enclosing restore owner retires the rejected candidate. This predicate does not itself enable public activation or certify the remaining logical dependencies.

## Native concrete routine reference validation

Let $F$ map each live stored routine's nonzero immutable object identity to its original declaration. SQL's `NativeRoutineReferences` constructs this borrowed map under the input allowance and rejects missing, zero or repeated identities. Let $B$ be the concrete function bindings selected from native column/default/generated/CHECK expressions, domain defaults and checks, partition-key expressions, views, stored SQL actions and bound SQL plans, including ordinary FROM functions and ROWS FROM groups, together with direct trigger identities and recorded rule-routine dependencies. Builtin calls, structural dispatch and retained resolution errors preserve their existing dispatch semantics; unresolved calls and deferred source bodies are outside this concrete-reference predicate and retain their existing runtime contract.

For every concrete binding $b$ in $B$, acceptance requires that $F(b.id)$ exists, its function/procedure kind matches the call, and SQL's ordinary `routine_signature_matches_with_control` accepts its original declared input signature. This is exactly the identity-and-signature predicate used by bound routine selection. Names are historical display metadata once an immutable identity is present: renaming cannot change the selected declaration, whereas deleting and recreating the same spelling cannot satisfy the original identity. The predicate examines all retained expression branches, including branches that ordinary execution can leave unevaluated. It neither evaluates a branch nor replaces an error with a new binding.

Direct trigger references additionally require the selected zero-input function to return the `trigger` pseudotype and use the supported trigger language. PostgreSQL 18 admits both scalar and SETOF declarations of that same pseudotype; the declaration flag remains unchanged, and trigger invocation still consumes the returned event row. Definition, native restoration and catalog projection use the same SQL-owned predicate, and routine compilation applies the same type classification when rejecting declared trigger arguments. This strengthens the accepted catalog domain without evaluating the deferred body or replacing its identity. The independent [PostgreSQL 18.4 fixture](../../tests/parity/pg18/backup_trigger_contract_oracle.sql) preserves renamed identities, wrong-result/argument/language diagnostics and successful SETOF trigger execution.

For a legacy trigger with no function identity, let `T` map the decoded schema/name pair of each zero-input nonprocedure declaration to that original borrowed declaration. Construction admits every ordered-map node before publication and rejects duplicate keys; lookups compare decoded components, so equivalent quoting is preserved without a search path. Acceptance requires its original qualified name to select exactly one declaration in `T` satisfying the shared handler contract. A concrete identity still takes precedence over display spelling. Neither path changes the stored optional identity. Both indexes retire their original reservations on failure or completion. At invocation, the Execution activation owner classifies trigger handlers as returning one event row even when their retained declaration has a SETOF flag; ordinary set-returning routines retain their existing activation kind. Thus successful validation and subsequent handler execution preserve the original event-row observation rather than discarding it as a set result.

Expression and plan traversal borrow existing nodes. Stored SQL actions use the existing admitted borrowed lowering and immediately discard its temporary plan; lowering preserves concrete bindings and performs no overload resolution or execution. Each map insertion and temporary plan allocation is admitted before allocation and retains its original cancellation. Execution supplies declarations and references from one immutable snapshot; the private Engine assembler only supplies that snapshot and owns candidate retirement. On success the candidate's declarations, bodies, evaluated values and ownership are unchanged, so all existing image correspondences and query compositions are preserved. On any missing identity, signature mismatch, cancellation or quota error, the predicate returns no candidate and releases its temporary map and plans. The original image owners then retire with the private Engine. Complete native-image acceptance additionally requires the other catalog and external-resource predicates; this component alone does not certify full restoration.

## Native catalog namespace validation

Let $N(C)$ be the namespaces visible in one immutable catalog $C$: its declared schemas, named graph owners and the three virtual system schemas recognized by the existing catalog namespace predicate. No session temporary namespace is synthesized. Let $D(C)$ contain each ordinary table, foreign table, view, sequence, index, domain, enum, composite, shell, range, independently named multirange and stored routine declaration. Native namespace acceptance requires $schema(d) \in N(C)$ for every $d \in D(C)$. Structured identities supply the original schema directly; routine names use SQL's admitted bound-name decoder, which preserves quoted components and requires an explicit schema instead of consulting a search path.

Execution visits every declaration category in $D(C)$ and applies the existing immutable namespace predicate to its original schema. Thus acceptance is equivalent to this membership condition: every visited declaration must satisfy it, and a candidate satisfying it passes every membership check. A named graph provides its ordinary namespace even without a separately declared schema; virtual system schemas remain implicit. A multirange is checked separately because its schema can differ from that of its associated range. The check neither invents a missing `public` schema nor substitutes a session temporary namespace.

Validation borrows all definitions and changes none of the native owners, identities, values or bindings. Consequently it is the identity on the state of every accepted candidate and preserves every previously established image observation and operation composition. Routine source bodies remain deferred; no SQL, callback or external resource is executed or opened. Each declaration checks cancellation, and bound-name scratch is admitted before allocation and released before the next routine. Failure retires scratch and the enclosing private Engine retires the rejected input owners. This predicate establishes namespace membership only; name uniqueness, remaining constraint and stored-expression relationships, external-resource compatibility and complete public restoration require their separate predicates.

## Native relation and type name uniqueness

Let $R$ and $T$ be the relation-name and type-name claim sets for the supplied ordinary catalog declarations, with each key an exact pair of decoded schema and local name. A plain relation contributes its key only to $R$. A table, foreign table, view or standalone composite contributes its key to both sets and its original array name to $T$. A scalar or range-family type contributes its own key and array key to $T$, while a shell contributes only its own key. The multirange and its array use the multirange's independent identity. Fixed system relation names, builtin scalar/array/pseudo-type names and fixed catalog domain names retain the addresses defined by their existing SQL owners.

SQL admits one ordered map per claim set. Before each insertion, the candidate key must not name a fixed member of that set; insertion must also find no earlier claim. By induction after any successful prefix, each map contains exactly the distinct admitted claims of that prefix. Therefore a complete successful traversal is equivalent to uniqueness within each supplied claim set and disjointness from its fixed members. A sequence or index can share a key with a scalar type because they occupy different sets, whereas a row-bearing relation cannot. This implements the existing creation namespace distinction without inventing a relation for a scalar type.

Recorded array names remain exact, including names previously displaced by ordinary declarations. An absent legacy array name uses the same underscore prefix and UTF-8 clipping operation as the owning array-name function, with admitted output; validation neither chooses another available name nor displaces an existing one. Structured key pairs keep embedded dots and quotes distinct without concatenation or search-path lookup. All copied key bytes and ordered-map nodes are admitted before allocation and retire with the private validator on success, cancellation, denial or conflicting input.

Execution supplies the ordinary and foreign tables, views, sequences, indexes and five native type registries from one immutable snapshot. Validation is the identity on every accepted declaration, its physical identity, artifact and native ownership. It therefore preserves the established image correspondences and existing query/transaction compositions for candidates satisfying this predicate and the remaining activation preconditions. The Engine adapter only supplies the selected snapshot and retires rejected private state. This predicate does not establish constraint/index ownership, stored-expression closure, routine relationships or external-resource compatibility.

## Native index ownership validation

SQL owns the native index predicate in `catalog/index/native.rs` and admitted stored-metadata projection in `catalog/index/stored/input.rs`. Execution supplies original ordinary-table and index rows from one immutable `CatalogReadSnapshot`; private Engine reconstruction applies this predicate before returning the candidate. This predicate validates recorded table, constraint and parent-index ownership. Index-expression/type semantics, physical-resource correspondence and complete public restoration remain separate requirements.

Let a captured catalog be `C = (T, K, I)`, where `T` maps exact relation names to original table incarnations and hierarchy declarations, `K` contains their original key constraints, and `I` contains original index rows. A recorded index address identifies one index, one table incarnation and one physical key. Let `P(C)` require: every index names an existing same-schema table; each recorded address is valid and its table incarnation matches; index identities and index OIDs are unique, and physical keys are unique within each table; every recorded owning-constraint edge names that table's original key with the same recorded enforcing-index identity, uniqueness/null semantics and ordered key/included columns; every recorded constraint-to-index edge has the matching reverse owner; and each parent-index edge names an index on the original partition parent. At most one index owns each constraint, and parent-index edges are acyclic. Legacy absent addresses introduce no synthesized identities or edges.

The admitted table and constraint maps contain exactly their supplied immutable declarations; duplicate or invalid original identities reject. Each index row uses SQL's original bound-name decoder and existing `IndexCatalogIdentity::validate` and `IndexRelationships::validate` predicates. Thus successful index registration establishes its original table incarnation and unique address. The second traversal checks every outgoing owner and parent edge, then the constraint traversal checks every recorded reverse edge. Key-list comparison decodes one original string at a time and compares exact ordered columns; repeated included columns remain legal and significant. These exhaustive finite traversals establish the corresponding clauses of `P(C)` without choosing a replacement object or evaluating stored expressions.

Each index has at most one parent. An iterative walk marks each newly visited index active; reaching another active node proves a directed cycle, while reaching a finished node or absent parent terminates the chain. A second walk marks that chain finished. Each new node is visited at most once in each walk, so acceptance is equivalent to acyclicity and traversal requires no recursive stack or population-sized path copy. Node colors belong to private admitted validation scratch and never alter `C`.

Stored JSON is consumed by Core's existing iterative grammar. Field names and physical keys use its admitted string decoder, fixed-size identities are decoded directly, and expression/type payloads remain slices of the original catalog text. Required, duplicate, unknown and mistyped ownership fields reject; this projection does not claim to validate the borrowed expression/type semantics. Workspace is bounded by admitted map nodes, copied physical keys and JSON nesting. The grammar and collection loops poll cancellation, and every temporary reservation retires on success or failure. No payload is allocated first and charged afterward.

The successful transformation is the identity on `C`: it neither changes names, addresses, constraints, predicates, physical artifacts or rows nor runs SQL, triggers or callbacks. Consequently, assuming the remaining restoration predicates and native data owners satisfy their contracts, ordinary catalog observations and subsequent index-enforced operations have the same inputs and meanings as before capture. Failed validation exposes no Engine and retires the private candidate through its existing owner boundary; it cannot repair or partially publish a catalog. This preserves composition because the successful map is the identity on the accepted carrier, while rejection is outside the publication domain. It does not assert that this ownership predicate alone proves complete restored-database validity.

Plain index keys and INCLUDE attributes also close over the captured row layout. Let `L(t)` be the set of live column names for each table with a declared layout; an empty declared layout has an empty set, whereas a document table without a declared layout has no such restriction. SQL admits one lookup entry for each `(table incarnation, column name)` and rejects duplicate live declarations. Its existing stored-list decoder visits every plain key and every included attribute, retaining exact decoded spelling and list order. Each visited name must belong to `L(t)` when that set exists, and every included attribute must be a column. Validation precedes optional index-address processing, so an addressless legacy row cannot omit these checks. Repeated key and included names remain valid occurrences of the same member.

The lookup contains exactly the supplied live names, and the decoder visits each relevant list element once; acceptance is therefore equivalent to this membership predicate. It neither adds a column nor rebinds or evaluates an expression. The map and decoded-name reservations retire on acceptance, denial or cancellation, independently of the unchanged catalog owners. For every previously valid image satisfying the predicate, the transformation remains the identity and preserves index names, contents, query inputs and subsequent write inputs. Expression-key/predicate typing and physical-artifact correspondence remain separate prerequisites for complete activation.

## Native constraint reference validation

SQL's `schema/constraints/native.rs` owns the predicate over original row layouts, key/CHECK declarations, foreign-key bindings and referenced-partition rows. Execution supplies ordinary and foreign definitions and original index rows from one immutable catalog; Engine supplies that snapshot and the existing private-candidate lifetime. The predicate performs no DDL, migration, constraint evaluation, trigger or callback execution. Complete expression typing, partition-bound compatibility and the remaining catalog/resource closure are additional activation requirements.

The compact [PostgreSQL 18.4 fixture](../../tests/parity/pg18/backup_constraint_shapes_oracle.sql) and [captured outcomes](../../tests/parity/pg18/backup_constraint_shapes_oracle.expected.txt) establish that repeated local and SET-action columns are accepted, while repeated referenced columns return `42830`. SQL owner tests retain those distinctions; the actual Engine archive test preserves repeated columns, reordered target keys, renamed indexes, nested referenced partitions and a NOT VALID CHECK with preexisting invalid rows before executing later valid and rejected writes.

Let `C = (T, A, K, E, F, I, H)` contain relation incarnations `T`, their live attributes `A`, key constraints `K`, CHECK and partition-key expressions `E`, foreign keys `F`, stored indexes `I` and partition metadata `H`. Let `P(C)` require unique relation/attribute lookup keys; nonempty key columns drawn without repetition from their own live layout, with included columns drawn from the same layout and repetitions permitted; every column variable in `E` names a live attribute or the supported `tableoid` system attribute; every foreign key has equally sized nonempty local/target column vectors, with distinct target columns; every target is an original ordinary relation whose selected index retains its original incarnation, table owner, uniqueness, absence of a predicate and exact set of ordinary target columns; and each paired operand has the ordinary SQL foreign-key comparison type. Local and SET-action columns may repeat, as verified independently on PostgreSQL 18.4. Explicit SET-action columns must belong to the local key. PERIOD keys retain their original ordered WITHOUT OVERLAPS constraint and matching period types/actions. Each recorded derived partition row resolves its original partition incarnation through its actual captured parent, in parent-before-child order.

The validator first admits maps containing exactly the borrowed original relation and attribute entries and addressed index rows. Duplicate entries reject; no lookup synthesizes a replacement. It then visits each key, expression and foreign key. Live-column lookups establish key and expression membership. The foreign-key traversal checks the arity and distinct target set, selects only the recorded index ID, and decodes each stored ordinary index key once under the supplied allowance. Each decoded key must match a previously unseen target column, and final cardinality must agree. This establishes equality of the target and index column sets while preserving permitted target permutations and repeated referencing columns. The shared controlled SQL operand resolver establishes the same directional coercion as ordinary DDL; all copied types and canonical names retire with its result. PERIOD checks restrict that same recorded index to the original ordered temporal key. Finally, the derived-row traversal admits visited partition IDs and requires every explicit parent to precede its child, and every stored parent relationship to agree with the original table hierarchy. These finite traversals establish exactly `P(C)`.

For accepted inputs, the result is the identity map on `C` and on all row/index/resource owners. No check expression or stored row is evaluated, so an unvalidated CHECK may retain previously invalid rows and remains unvalidated; later writes still use its original runtime enforcement. Names retained for diagnostics cannot substitute another index lifetime. Because validation changes no declaration or observation, its successful composition with the native image inverses preserves their established catalog and query meanings, conditional on the remaining activation predicates. Rejection publishes no Engine and releases the same private candidate rather than repairing a live catalog. Admitted maps, controlled syntax traversal, string decoding and comparison types enforce the supplied allowance and cancellation; their reservations retire on success or any failure.

### Constraint catalog identity closure

Ordinary tables, foreign tables, domains and constraint triggers own rows in the same `pg_constraint` namespace. Let `R` be the disjoint occurrence sequence of complete table/domain identities and partial or complete constraint-trigger identities, including NOT NULL, column/table CHECK, primary/unique keys, foreign keys, derived referenced-partition rows and constraint triggers. A foreign key's enforcement-family identity is not its catalog-row identity, and repeated hierarchy provenance describes the existing row rather than an additional row. Index and trigger-row OIDs belong to separate catalogs; a constraint trigger contributes its constraint OID and original incarnation, while an ordinary trigger contributes no constraint row. Each supplied trigger OID and incarnation contributes independently, even when its companion field is absent. Legacy absence of an identity remains absent; this predicate neither infers nor materializes one.

SQL's borrowed identity iterators enumerate `R` without copying expressions. The native validator admits two maps, keyed respectively by the supplied original incarnation and OID, and rejects an invalid field or a repeated key before recording the next row. Each projection is partial: a missing field contributes no key, and a supplied key is checked exactly once regardless of its companion. Induction over the prefix of `R` proves that success is equivalent to validity and injectivity of both partial projections on every supplied row. Execution lends all four owner families from the same immutable snapshot, so collisions across families cannot escape the predicate. The independent PostgreSQL 18.4 fixture above retains distinct catalog rows for inherited foreign keys and one constraint OID namespace for relation, domain and constraint-trigger rows, with the separate trigger row linked by `tgconstraint`.

The predicate leaves declarations, identities, validation flags and expressions unchanged. A shared foreign-key enforcement family therefore remains shared while its distinct catalog rows remain distinct, and equal OIDs in different catalog classes remain admissible. Each map insertion obtains the original allowance before allocation and observes the original cancellation token; all scratch retires before subsequent reference validation or on any failure. The operation adds no query algebra or optimizer rewrite. Missing legacy identity completion, remaining partition-bound compatibility and expression/resource consistency remain separate activation predicates.

### Complete referenced-partition membership

Let `G` be the captured ordinary-table partition graph and `F(t)` the set of supplied foreign-key enforcement families on relation `t`. SQL's declaration publisher and native validator share the borrowed family iterator; neither a historical name nor a missing family identity substitutes for membership. A foreign key on partition `t` is inherited exactly when its supplied family belongs to `F(parent(t))`. The native predicate requires an inherited copy to have no referenced-partition rows, matching the existing declaration publisher. The independent PostgreSQL 18.4 fixture records three rows for the declaring foreign key over a two-level referenced tree and one row for its referencing-partition copy.

For a declaring foreign key targeting `r`, let `D` be its recorded derived rows. The existing parent-before-child traversal proves that each row in `D` names a distinct non-root descendant and retains its actual parent in `G`. The added predicate visits the children of `r` and of every member of `D` and requires every child to belong to `D`. The empty path begins at `r`; induction on path length proves that every proper descendant of `r` belongs to `D`. Together these directions establish equality with the entire referenced subtree, including an empty tree, while excluding unrelated partitions. A reachable cycle cannot satisfy both parent-before-child order and complete membership without repeating a row or including the forbidden root.

The validator builds admitted parent/child links once from the immutable catalog and checks membership without regenerating names, identities, flags or declarations. Each sibling link refers to a previously inserted child, so its finite traversal needs no recursion or unbounded call stack. Scratch is proportional to captured partition edges, supplied families and the selected foreign key's derived rows; map insertion and traversal retain the original memory allowance and cancellation. Failed construction exposes no partial index, and every exit retires its reservations. Successful validation is the identity on catalog values and therefore preserves the original foreign-key observations and query compositions. Definition equivalence within an inherited family and other expression/resource predicates retain their separate obligations.

### Complete referencing-partition foreign-key families

SQL's inheritance owner lends the same attachment definition to ordinary foreign-key attachment and native restoration: ordered local columns, original target relation, ordered referenced columns, PERIOD semantics, enforceability, deferrability, initial deferral, update/delete actions and match type. Names, independent catalog identities, validation flags, selected eligible indexes and SET-action column subsets are not attachment-equivalence fields. The PostgreSQL 18.4 fixture retains a child with a distinct name and SET subset under the original parent's `conparentid`; each native index and action subset still satisfies its separate reference predicate.

Let `F(t, f)` denote the borrowed attachment definition with supplied enforcement-family identity `f` on relation `t`, and let `children(t)` be its captured immediate referencing partitions. Require at most one `F(t, f)` per pair, one declaring root for each `f`, and `F(c, f) = F(t, f)` for every `c` in `children(t)` whenever `F(t, f)` exists. A declaring root is a relation whose immediate partition parent does not hold `f`. These predicates apply to supplied family identities; they neither invent absent legacy identities nor substitute catalog-row identity for enforcement-family identity.

The admitted family map registers every original column/table foreign key once and rejects a duplicate pair. Iterating its entries checks each declaring-root claim and every immediate child lookup. Missing children and unequal definitions reject. With the separately established acyclic partition relation, induction on partition depth proves complete and equivalent family membership below each unique declaring root. An unrelated relation cannot reuse the family identity, because its distinct root claim collides. The comparison is shared with ordinary attachment, so restoration does not impose stronger definition equality on a legitimately attached child.

All family definitions borrow immutable declarations. Family and root lookup nodes use the original production allowance, sibling traversal polls cancellation, and scratch retires on success, allocation denial or cancellation. The accepted transformation is the identity on all declarations, names, validation states, selected indexes, original catalog addresses and data. Thus family-dependent enforcement and deferred-event grouping retain their original inputs when composed with the separate reference, hierarchy, expression and resource predicates. Rejection cannot publish or repair a private candidate; this predicate alone does not establish complete restore validity.

## Native sequence dependency validation

Let $R$ be the supplied table registry, let $C$ map each live pair of table and column object identities to the original column declaration, and let $S$ be the set of original sequence relation identities. Execution supplies ordinary and foreign columns from the same immutable catalog as the sequences. SQL admits indexes of these borrowed values, rejecting duplicate owner-table identities, duplicate column identities within a table and duplicate sequence names. An explicit sequence owner must resolve by its original pair in $C$; reusing a display name cannot satisfy an absent or replaced incarnation. An internal owner additionally requires that the referenced column retains identity generation and names that exact sequence. Automatic ownership retains its existing ability to refer to an otherwise ordinary column.

Every live implicit column sequence reference must already be canonical and belong to $S$. Inherited SERIAL and identity declarations may reference another table's sequence; the check does not substitute a local sequence or change ownership. Legacy table counters without a sequence remain valid, whereas an unconsumed identity declaration or a nonlegacy implicit column without a sequence rejects validation. Historical provenance names do not replace stable explicit owner identities. The validator neither infers legacy owners nor changes current sequence positions, cache reservations, allocation generations or values.

For any candidate satisfying these predicates, validation is the identity on both $C$ and all sequence records: it borrows declarations, checks set membership and equality, and discards only newly admitted scratch. Thus native import preserves the original owner relationships, inherited value source and autonomous sequence observations already established by the sequence image inverse. Each table, column and sequence is visited a constant number of times apart from bounded name decoding and ordered lookup, using $O((|R|+|C|+|S|)\log(1+|R|+|C|+|S|))$ work and $O(|R|+|C|+|S|)$ workspace. Original cancellation and quota failure release the indexes without publishing or mutating a candidate. Captured named sequence-function references use the [additional argument predicate](#native-sequence-function-reference-validation); remaining expression closure and complete public restoration remain additional requirements.


## Native sequence function reference validation

SQL observes captured named arguments of selected `nextval`, `currval` and `setval` builtins through borrowed AST and scalar traversal. The resolved function binding takes precedence over a historical display name; user functions, host dispatches and retained resolution errors do not become builtin calls. Direct literal names and their `regclass` casts identify names; explicit text casts and already typed numeric values retain runtime resolution. Numeric literal spellings use the shared SQL-owned native regclass classifier and do not imply live relation membership: `-` denotes OID zero, digit strings retain native radix and uint32 bounds, and signed spellings remain names. Execution uses the same classifier; neither path substitutes decimal parsing for the native rule. The independent [PostgreSQL 18.4 fixture](../../tests/parity/pg18/backup_sequence_arguments_oracle.sql) and [captured outcomes](../../tests/parity/pg18/backup_sequence_arguments_oracle.expected.txt) establish that missing numeric OIDs and nonsequence relation OIDs can be stored and rejected only on execution.

Let `C` be the immutable catalog, `N(C)` its ordinary, foreign, view, index, sequence, virtual and graph-label relation namespace, and `E(C)` the named references reported by the selected-builtin visitor over stored column/domain expressions, partition keys, views, bound SQL routines, trigger conditions and rule actions/conditions. Execution uses the same catalog-expression inventory as the concrete routine predicate. The new predicate is `P(C) = ∀ n ∈ E(C), canonical(n) ∈ N(C)`. SQL decodes a canonical name without a search path; Execution performs membership lookup in that same snapshot. Each reported name is checked before the finite traversal succeeds, so success establishes `P(C)`. No relation-kind restriction is added: the operation retains its ordinary execution-time kind check. Deferred routine source bodies are not compiled, and an arbitrary numeric value cannot prove that an original named dependency existed. This predicate does not establish closure for index expression payloads or all remaining catalog/resource relationships.

The successful result is the identity on `C`, expression trees and sequence state. Traversal neither resolves an overload, rewrites a name, allocates a sequence value nor invokes a function. Thus its composition with the other native predicates and image inverses preserves all existing observations on the accepted carrier, conditional on their remaining contracts. In particular, deferred errors remain deferred and same-named user functions retain their selected identities. Borrowed traversal avoids copying unrelated literal payloads; canonical decoding and stored-action lowering reserve their workspace through the original production allowance and poll both cancellation owners. Failure retires that scratch and the enclosing private candidate without publication. Complete bounded production restoration and final-provider qualification remain additional requirements.

## Native event relation validation

Let `C` be the selected immutable catalog, `N(C)` its relation namespace and `A(C, r)` the declared live columns of relation `r`. Document tables without a closed declaration retain their open field domain. Let `E(C)` contain the original event registry entries, recorded rule relation/column dependencies, concrete sources and mutation targets in stored rule actions, and references in both captured condition syntax and condition plans. Native validation does not infer a missing legacy dependency summary or compile a deferred routine source body.

For every event entry, the predicate requires the decoded declaration owner and name to equal its registry key and the owner to exist with the ordinary event relation kind. Trigger kind checks share the definition owner's predicate; a constraint trigger's referenced relation must be an ordinary table, and each UPDATE column must belong to its owner's column domain. Every recorded rule relation must belong to `N(C)` and every recorded column pair `(r, a)` must satisfy `r ∈ N(C)` and `a ∈ A(C, r)`. Concrete rule sources and mutation targets must name canonical members of `N(C)` even when the legacy dependency summary is absent. SQL owns these requirements; Execution lends captured ordinary, foreign and view columns and the existing system/graph layouts. Ordinary source binding and native validation share SQL's fixed sequence column names.

Rule traversal reuses the existing admitted syntax lowering and stored-plan reference visitor. A leaf table source is reported unless it names a visible CTE; a mutation target is always reported. For a nonrecursive CTE, induction over declaration order gives exactly the outer and preceding names; a recursive CTE enters all sibling names before its bodies. The visitor pushes every child query, scalar-subquery plan and mutation input, so structural induction establishes that all concrete relation leaves are reported. OLD/NEW scalar references are expression nodes, not relation sources; they add no table edge. `FROM new` still requires a real qualified relation or a local CTE and does not acquire an implicit pseudo-table. The [PostgreSQL 18.4 fixture](../../tests/parity/pg18/backup_event_relations_oracle.sql) and [captured results](../../tests/parity/pg18/backup_event_relations_oracle.expected.txt) verify owner/dependency retention after a rename, subsequent rule effects and the original missing relation/column outcomes.

The validator returns success only after every selected entry and edge satisfies its predicate. It has no mutable catalog access, and lowering produces only private admitted scratch, so successful validation is exactly the identity on `C`, all stored expressions and all retained data. Composition with another native predicate therefore preserves every observation and algebraic operation already defined on their accepted carrier. No optimizer rewrite or new query operator is introduced. Canonical names and lowered plans retain the original production allowance; each finite traversal polls the original cancellation scopes, and every return retires its scratch. Failure reaches the existing private activation boundary without publishing an event or invoking a callback. Expression typing, complete dependency-summary equivalence and remaining routine/resource closure are separate obligations.


## Resumable native value input

Core's `ImageDecoder` retains one admitted pinned codec continuation and one fixed 4,096-byte input channel. `ImageDecodeWindow` independently limits source bytes and continuation polls per call; both limits must be positive. The decoder borrows the source only during an advance. Codecs await native input primitives or explicit checkpoints; an unrelated pending operation is invalid. `ValueImageInput` uses this owner to retain incomplete primitive fields and an admitted iterative container stack. `read_value_image` drives the same parser on its invoking stack, preserving allocation-free scalar input. A single value consumes exactly its own encoding and leaves enclosing stream bytes unread.

Let the existing native image grammar define a deterministic decoder D, and let a suspended state consist of the next grammar operation, its admitted partial fields, the enclosing container stack, and either an empty channel or one original read response. Projection consumes any buffered response before the next source read. Initially this is D at the version header. A read transition supplies only the requested prefix, in original order; an interrupted attempt supplies no bytes. A checkpoint changes no grammar state. A container transition pushes the next unfinished parent or transfers one completed child to that same parent, which is the explicit-stack counterpart of D's recursive return. Induction over these transitions therefore preserves D's next operation, byte position and exact partial values for every partition of the input into positive windows. Completion yields the original native value, including opaque descriptors, float bits and array metadata.
